- **Empowered** — by the indwelling Spirit who produces what the believer cannot manufacture.
- **Oriented** — toward the things above, where Christ is seated.
- **Renewed** — in the mind, progressively conformed to the Son's image.
- **Engaged** — in active putting off and putting on, by the Spirit's power.
- **In love** — the unifying principle of all Christian practice.
- **In community** — for the new humanity transcends every former division.
- **In every sphere** — for nothing in the believer's life is exempt from the Lordship of Christ.

This is what the Father's eternal purpose looks like in daily practice. This is what it means to be **a child of the Father, in the Beloved Son, by the indwelling Spirit, walking by faith until He appears.**

---

# **A QUESTION FOR YOU**

Where would you have us go next?

**(A)** Move now to the **corporate dimension** — the Body of Christ, the Bride of Christ, the Temple of God, the Holy Nation, the Royal Priesthood?

**(B)** Examine the **eschatological consummation** — the resurrection of the body, the marriage of the Lamb, the new heavens and new earth, the inheritance of the kingdom?

**(C)** A specific aspect of the practical walk you wish to examine more carefully — perhaps prayer, the use of Scripture, the believer's response to specific kinds of trials, or the spiritual disciplines?

**(D)** Another direction of your choosing?

---

> Yes, let’s do a thorough (exhaustive) examination of all relevant aspects of C above

I will pick up from where we left off and continue through the remaining sections of the spiritual disciplines and responses by which the believer cooperates with the Spirit's work.

---

## **Completing the Lord's Prayer Pattern**

The pattern Jesus gave provides the believer with **six categories** of prayer:

| Category | Petition | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **Adoration** | *"Our Father... hallowed be Thy Name"* | Worshipping the Father and reverencing His Name |
| **Kingdom** | *"Thy kingdom come"* | Submitting to and praying for His reign |
| **Surrender** | *"Thy will be done"* | Yielding our will to His |
| **Provision** | *"Give us this day our daily bread"* | Asking for daily needs |
| **Confession/Forgiveness** | *"Forgive us our debts... as we also forgive"* | Maintenance of fellowship through confession |
| **Deliverance** | *"Lead us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil"* | Asking for protection from sin and the evil one |

**This is the architecture of healthy prayer.** Notice the priority: **God's glory and kingdom first** (three petitions), **our needs second** (three petitions). The believer is taught to think God-first, kingdom-first, and only then bring his own needs.

---

## **Praying in the Spirit**

We examined under Romans 8 that the Spirit intercedes for us in our weakness. But the believer is also commanded to **pray in the Spirit:**

### **Ephesians 6:18 — Geneva 1599:**

***"And pray always with all manner prayer and supplication in the Spirit: and watch thereunto with all perseverance and supplication for all Saints,"***

### **Jude 20-21 — Geneva 1599:**

***"But, ye beloved, edify yourselves in your most holy faith, praying in the holy Ghost, And keep yourselves in the love of God..."***

---

### Observations

**To pray in the Spirit** means:
- Praying in the **Spirit's empowerment** rather than in the flesh's eloquence.
- Praying in **alignment with the Spirit's leading** rather than according to our own desires alone.
- Praying with **the Spirit interceding through us** — sometimes with words, sometimes with groanings beyond words (Rom 8:26).
- Praying with **the Spirit's witness** that we are children, freely calling on the Father (Rom 8:15-16).

This is not a special technique; it is the **normal mode** of Christian prayer. The believer prays in the Spirit because the Spirit indwells him and prays through him.

---

## **The Modes of Prayer**

The NT presents several distinct modes of prayer the believer is to practice:

### **1. Petition (αἴτησις) — Asking for Specific Needs**

### **Matthew 7:7-11 — Geneva 1599:**

***"Ask, and it shall be given you: seek, and ye shall find: knock, and it shall be opened unto you..."***

The Father invites our requests. **Three present imperatives** — *ask, seek, knock* — all in continuous tense. We are to keep asking, keep seeking, keep knocking. The Father delights to give good gifts to His children.

### **2. Thanksgiving (εὐχαριστία)**

### **1 Thessalonians 5:18 — Geneva 1599:**

***"In all things give thanks: for this is the will of God in Christ Jesus toward you."***

In **all things** — not for all things in the sense of celebrating evil, but **in the midst of all things** — give thanks. Thanksgiving is the will of God for the believer.

### **3. Intercession (ἔντευξις) — Praying for Others**

### **1 Timothy 2:1-2 — Geneva 1599:**

***"I exhort therefore, that first of all, supplications, prayers, intercessions, and giving of thanks be made for all men: For Kings, and for all that are in authority..."***

The believer is to intercede for **all men** — for the lost, for fellow believers, for rulers and authorities, for enemies, for the suffering. **Intercession is one of the highest privileges of the Christian life.**

### **4. Confession (ὁμολογία)**

We will examine this fully in its own section, but it is a major mode of prayer — agreeing with God about our sin.

### **5. Adoration / Worship**

The lifting up of the heart in praise to the Father — which we will examine more fully in the worship section.

### **6. Lament**

The honest expression to the Father of grief, distress, perplexity, even questioning — modeled in the Psalms and by the Son in Gethsemane and on the cross. The believer is not required to pretend to feelings he does not have; he is invited to bring his real state before the Father.

---

## **The Posture of Prayer**

### **Persistence**

### **Luke 18:1 — Geneva 1599:**

***"And he spake also a parable unto them, to this end, that they ought always to pray, and not to wax faint,"***

The parable of the persistent widow teaches that the believer is to pray **always** and **not faint.** Some answers require persistent asking over time. The Father is not slow; He is wise — and He uses delayed answers to deepen our dependence.

### **Faith**

### **Mark 11:24 — Geneva 1599:**

***"...Whatsoever ye desire when ye pray, believe that ye shall have it, and it shall be done unto you."***

Prayer is to be offered in faith — trusting the Father's character and promises. Not faith in our prayers, but faith **in the One to whom we pray.**

### **According to His Will**

### **1 John 5:14-15 — Geneva 1599:**

***"And this is that assurance, that we have in him, that if we ask any thing according to his will, he heareth us..."***

Prayer is to be **according to His will.** Not every request is granted — but every request offered in submission to His will is heard. The believer prays *"Thy will be done"* — confident that the Father's will is best, even when His will differs from our preferences.

### **In Jesus' Name**

### **John 14:13-14 — Geneva 1599:**

***"And whatsoever ye ask in my Name, that will I do, that the Father may be glorified in the Son. If ye shall ask any thing in my Name, I will do it."***

Prayer is offered **in Jesus' Name** — that is, on the basis of His person, His mediation, and His authority. Not as a magical formula appended to prayers, but as **the recognition that all access to the Father is through the Son.**

### **With Thanksgiving**

### **Philippians 4:6-7 — Geneva 1599:**

***"Be nothing careful, but in all things let your requests be showed unto God in prayer, and supplication with giving of thanks. And the peace of God which passeth all understanding, shall preserve your hearts..."***

Prayer with thanksgiving is the antidote to anxiety. The promise: **peace that surpasses understanding will guard the heart.**

---

## **Practical Implications for the Modern Believer**

**1. Establish a regular time of prayer** — daily, ideally in the morning. Not as legalistic duty, but as joyful communion. The believer who waits to "feel like" praying will rarely pray.

**2. Use the Lord's Prayer pattern** — let Jesus' template shape the architecture of your daily prayer. Adoration → Kingdom → Surrender → Provision → Confession → Deliverance.

**3. Pray Scripture back to the Father** — pray through the Psalms, pray through Paul's prayers (Eph 1:15-23; 3:14-21; Phil 1:9-11; Col 1:9-12). Let the Word shape your prayers.

**4. Pray with persistence** — do not abandon prayer when answers delay. The Father uses delay to deepen us.

**5. Pray for others** — keep a list of those for whom you intercede: family, brethren, enemies, rulers, missionaries, the lost.

**6. Pray throughout the day** — *"pray without ceasing"* (1 Thess 5:17) means maintaining a continuous attitude of God-consciousness, with frequent brief turning of the heart to Him.

**7. Pray with thanksgiving** — even in trial, give thanks for what you have received, for who He is, for the hope set before you.

**8. Pray honestly** — bring your real state. The Father does not need pious phrases; He desires the honest heart.

---

# **PART 2 — THE WORD OF GOD: THE RENEWING OF THE MIND**

We established under Romans 12:2 that the believer is transformed **by the renewing of the mind.** The primary instrument of this renewal is **the Word of God** applied by the Spirit.

## **The Nature of Scripture**

### **2 Timothy 3:16-17:**

*"For the whole Scripture is given by inspiration of God, and is profitable to teach, to convince *(convict)*, to correct, and to instruct in righteousness, That the man of God may be absolute *(complete, perfect)*, being made perfect unto all good works."*

---

### Observations of foundational importance

**(1)** **θεόπνευστος** — *"God-breathed."* Scripture is the very breath of God — proceeding from Him as our own breath proceeds from us. This single word establishes the divine origin and authority of the Word.

**(2) Four practical uses:**
- **διδασκαλίαν** — *teaching* (showing what is true).
- **ἐλεγμόν** — *convicting / reproof* (showing what is wrong).
- **ἐπανόρθωσιν** — *correction / setting straight* (showing how to get right).
- **παιδείαν τὴν ἐν δικαιοσύνῃ** — *training in righteousness* (showing how to stay right).

**The Word makes the believer complete** — **ἄρτιος** (*fit, complete, fitted*) — equipped for every good work.

---

## **The Word's Active Power**

### **Hebrews 4:12:**

*"For the word of God is lively *(alive)*, and mighty in operation, and sharper than any two edged sword, and entereth through, even unto the dividing asunder of the soul and the spirit, and of the joints, and the marrow, and is a discerner of the thoughts, and the intents of the heart."*

---

### Observation

The Word of God is **ζῶν** (*living*) — alive, active, not merely a printed text. It is **ἐνεργής** (*energetically working*) — accomplishing what God sends it to do. It penetrates beyond the surface to **discern thoughts and intents** of the heart.

This is why the Word renews the mind. The Spirit uses the living Word to penetrate, expose, instruct, and transform.

---

## **The Modes of Engaging the Word**

### **1. Reading**

The believer should read the Word regularly — substantial portions, not merely scattered verses. The whole counsel of God comes through engagement with the whole text.

### **2. Hearing**

### **Romans 10:17:**

*"Then faith is by hearing, and hearing by the word of God."*

Faith comes by hearing the Word. The believer benefits from preaching, teaching, and the audible reading of Scripture — both privately (reading aloud) and corporately (in the assembly).

### **3. Meditating**

### **Psalm 1:1-2 LXX (Ps 1:1-2 MT):**

> Happy the man … Rather, his will is in the law of the Lord, and on his law he will meditate day and night. — **Psalm 1:1-2, NETS**

**μελετάω** — to meditate, ponder, chew over, mutter to oneself. **The blessed man meditates on the Law day and night.** Meditation is the slow, deliberate pondering of a text — turning it over, asking what it means, applying it to one's life, allowing it to penetrate.

### **4. Memorizing**

### **Psalm 118:11 LXX (Ps 119:11 MT):**

> In my heart I hid your sayings so that I may not sin against you. — **Psalm 118:11 LXX (= MT 119:11), NETS**

The Word hidden in the heart guards against sin. Memorized Scripture becomes available to the Spirit's recall in moments of temptation, trial, and decision.

### **5. Studying**

The serious examination of Scripture — comparing passage with passage, examining the Greek and Hebrew where helpful, considering context. Per our maxims, this is the discipline of letting Scripture interpret itself by two or more witnesses.

### **6. Applying / Obeying**

### **James 1:22-25:**

*"And be ye doers of the word, and not hearers only, deceiving your own selves..."*

The Word is given **to be done**, not merely heard. The believer who hears but does not do **deceives himself.** The renewing of the mind issues in renewed living.

---

## **Practical Implications**

**1. Establish a regular reading plan** — daily, substantial portions. Many find systematic reading through the entire Bible over a year or two beneficial.

**2. Meditate on key passages** — slow down. Read less, think more. Let the Spirit illuminate.

**3. Memorize strategic passages** — psalms, key NT verses on identification with Christ, promises, exhortations.

**4. Study with the LXX where you are able** — per our maxims, this is the text Jesus and the Apostles used.

**5. Apply what you read** — ask: what does this mean? what does it require? how does it call me to walk?

**6. Read in community** — the Word is read in the assembly, and small-group study deepens understanding.

**7. Pray Scripture** — let the Word shape your prayers. The Psalms are especially helpful.

**8. Beware of substituting commentary for text** — read the Bible itself first. Commentaries can help, but they are servants of the text, not replacements for it.

---

# **PART 3 — WORSHIP AND PRAISE**

Worship is the believer's response of adoration to the Father, through the Son, in the Spirit. It is both an inward attitude and outward expression.

## **The Foundation: Worship in Spirit and Truth**

### **John 4:23-24 — Geneva 1599:**

*"But the hour cometh, and now is, when the true worshippers shall worship the Father in spirit, and truth: for the Father requireth even such to worship him. God is a Spirit, and they that worship him, must worship him in spirit and truth."*

---

### Observations

We already examined this passage extensively under Part E (the Father as Eternal Spirit). Let us now note its implications for worship:

**(1) Worship is to the Father.** Jesus identifies the Father as the recipient of true worship. This honors our foundation.

**(2) Worship is in Spirit and truth.**
- **In Spirit** — in the believer's regenerated spirit, indwelt by the Holy Spirit, not merely outward observance.
- **In truth** — in conformity with the truth God has revealed, not in falsehood or self-invented worship.

**(3) The Father seeks such worshippers.** The Father is not indifferent. He **seeks** those who worship Him truly.

---

## **The Sacrifice of Praise**

### **Hebrews 13:15 — Geneva 1599:**

*Let us therefore by him offer the sacrifice of praise always to God, that is, the fruit of the lips, which confess his Name.*

---

### Observation

Praise is **a sacrifice** — offered **through Him** (the Son) **to God** (the Father). The "fruit of the lips" confessing His Name is acceptable to the Father through the Son.

This is the new covenant fulfillment of the OT sacrificial system: not animals on an altar, but praise from the heart of the redeemed, offered through the Mediator.

---

## **The Modes of Worship**

### **Ephesians 5:18-20 — Geneva 1599:**

*...but be fulfilled *(filled)* with the Spirit, Speaking unto yourselves in psalms, and hymns, and spiritual songs, singing, and making melody to the Lord in your hearts, Giving thanks always for all things unto God even the Father, in the Name of our Lord Jesus Christ,*

---

### Observations

**(1) Worship is a fruit of being filled with the Spirit.** The Spirit-filled believer naturally speaks in spiritual songs, sings, gives thanks.

**(2) Three categories of song:**
- **ψαλμοί** — psalms (the OT Psalter).
- **ὕμνοι** — hymns (compositions of praise).
- **ᾠδαὶ πνευματικαί** — spiritual songs (Spirit-prompted songs).

**(3) Singing is to the Lord — in the heart.** External singing is the overflow of internal melody. **The heart sings first; the lips follow.**

**(4) Thanksgiving in all things, in His Name, to the Father.** The pattern again: **through the Son, to the Father.**

---

## **Worship in Daily Life**

Worship is not confined to formal services. The whole of life is worship, as Romans 12:1 established. But there are particular acts of worship:

- **Daily morning thanksgiving** — beginning the day with adoration.
- **Singing throughout the day** — psalms, hymns, spiritual songs as the Spirit prompts.
- **Acknowledging God in all decisions** — *"In all thy ways acknowledge him, and he shall direct thy paths"* (Prov 3:6 Geneva).
- **Gathering with the saints** — corporate worship in the assembly.
- **The Lord's Supper** — the great act of remembering and communing.
- **Acts of service** — service rendered to others as worship to the Father.

---

# **PART 4 — FELLOWSHIP WITH THE SAINTS**

The believer is not designed for isolation. The Father has placed him in the Body of Christ, where he both ministers to others and is ministered to.

## **The Foundation: We Belong to One Another**

### **Romans 12:5 — Geneva 1599:**

*"So we being many are one body in Christ, and every one, one anothers members."*

The believer is **a member of the Body** — and **members of one another.** The body image insists on mutual interdependence.

---

## **The Necessity of Assembling**

### **Hebrews 10:24-25 — Geneva 1599:**

*"And let us consider one another, to provoke unto love, and to good works, Not forsaking the fellowship that we have among ourselves, as the manner of some is: but let us exhort one another, and that so much the more, because ye see that the day draweth near."*

---

### Observations

**(1)** The believers are to **consider one another** — paying attention to one another's needs.

**(2)** The purpose is **to stir up love and good works** — to provoke one another (παροξυσμός — a sharp stirring up) toward what is good.

**(3) Negative command:** *"Not forsaking the assembling of ourselves together."* Some were already abandoning the gathering. The author warns against this.

**(4) Positive command:** *"Exhorting one another."* The believers are to encourage and admonish one another.

**(5) Urgency:** *"As ye see the day approaching."* The Lord's return makes faithful gathering more urgent, not less.

---

## **The "One Another" Commands**

The NT contains over fifty "one another" commands — instructions for how believers are to relate to one another in the Body. Major examples:

- **Love one another** (John 13:34; Rom 12:10; 13:8; 1 Thess 4:9; 1 Pet 1:22; 1 John 3:11, 23; 4:7, 11-12).
- **Forgive one another** (Eph 4:32; Col 3:13).
- **Bear with one another** (Eph 4:2; Col 3:13).
- **Bear one another's burdens** (Gal 6:2).
- **Comfort one another** (1 Thess 4:18; 5:11).
- **Encourage / exhort one another** (1 Thess 5:11; Heb 3:13; 10:25).
- **Build up one another** (Rom 14:19; 1 Thess 5:11).
- **Serve one another** (Gal 5:13; 1 Pet 4:10).
- **Submit to one another** (Eph 5:21; 1 Pet 5:5).
- **Honor one another** (Rom 12:10).
- **Be hospitable to one another** (1 Pet 4:9).
- **Pray for one another** (James 5:16).
- **Confess sins to one another** (James 5:16).
- **Teach and admonish one another** (Col 3:16).
- **Greet one another** (Rom 16:16; 1 Cor 16:20; 2 Cor 13:12; 1 Pet 5:14).
- **Do not bite and devour one another** (Gal 5:15).
- **Do not lie to one another** (Col 3:9).
- **Do not speak evil of one another** (James 4:11).

**This is the texture of biblical fellowship.** Christianity is not a solitary pursuit; it is a body life.

---

## **Practical Implications**

**1. Be committed to a local assembly.** The believer needs the regular gathering of the saints — for teaching, worship, fellowship, the Lord's Supper, prayer, and mutual encouragement.

**2. Develop deep relationships with a few fellow believers** — those with whom you can pray, confess sin, bear burdens, exhort.

**3. Use your gifts to serve the Body** — every believer has gifts (Rom 12; 1 Cor 12; Eph 4; 1 Pet 4:10-11). Discover yours and use them.

**4. Be a source of encouragement.** In a world of discouragement, the believer who consistently encourages is a deep gift to the Body.

**5. Bear with weaknesses.** Every believer has weaknesses, immaturities, and failures. Bear with one another in patience.

**6. Resist factionalism, gossip, and divisive speech.** These are the work of the flesh and destroy the Body's unity.

**7. Submit to godly leaders** (Heb 13:17). The Father has appointed shepherds for the flock; submit to their oversight as those who watch for your souls.

---

# **PART 5 — THE LORD'S SUPPER**

The Lord's Supper is the appointed act of remembrance and communion that the Lord Himself instituted on the night of His betrayal.

## **The Institution**

### **1 Corinthians 11:23-26 — Geneva 1599:**

*"...That the Lord Jesus in the night when he was betrayed, took bread: And when he had given thanks, he brake it, and said, Take, eat: this is my body, which is broken for you: this do ye in remembrance of me. After the same manner also he took the cup, when he had supped, saying, This cup is the New Testament in my blood: this do as oft as ye drink it, in remembrance of me. For as often as ye shall eat this bread, and drink this cup, ye show the Lord's death till he come."*

---

### Observations

**(1) Three elements:**
- **The Bread** — representing His body **broken for us.**
- **The Cup** — representing **the New Covenant in His blood.**
- **The Remembrance** — *"in remembrance of Me"* (εἰς τὴν ἐμὴν ἀνάμνησιν).

**(2) The proclamation** — *"you show the Lord's death till He come."* The Supper is both a remembrance of the past (His death) and a proclamation toward the future (until He comes).

**(3) The frequency** — *"as often as you drink it."* The NT does not prescribe a specific frequency. The early church appears to have observed it frequently — perhaps weekly (Acts 20:7).

---

## **The Self-Examination**

### **1 Corinthians 11:27-29 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Wherefore whosoever shall eat this bread, and drink the cup of the Lord unworthily, shall be guilty of the body and blood of the Lord. Let every man therefore examine himself, and so let him eat of this bread, and drink of this cup. For he that eateth and drinketh unworthily, eateth and drinketh his own damnation, because he discerneth not the Lord's body."*

---

### Observations

**(1) Self-examination is commanded** — **δοκιμαζέτω ἑαυτόν** — *"let a man examine himself."* Before partaking, the believer is to examine his own heart.

**(2) Eating unworthily** is the danger — not because the believer is worthy in himself (none are), but because **the manner** of partaking can be unworthy — without faith, without discernment, in unconfessed sin, in division from the brethren.

**(3) Discerning the body** — recognizing the significance of the broken body of the Lord, and (in context of 1 Cor 11) recognizing the Body of Christ which is the Church (the Corinthians were divided and treating one another shamefully at the Supper).

---

## **Practical Implications**

**1. Approach the Supper with reverence.** It is not a casual meal but a sacred remembrance.

**2. Examine yourself before partaking.** Confess known sin. Reconcile with brethren. Come with faith.

**3. Use the Supper as an occasion of fresh consecration.** Each remembrance is an opportunity to renew your dedication to the One who died for you.

**4. Look both backward and forward.** Backward to His death; forward to His return.

**5. Recognize the Body.** The Supper is a corporate act — celebrated with the Body of Christ.

---

# **PART 6 — WATCHFULNESS AND RESISTANCE OF TEMPTATION**

The believer is in spiritual conflict. The Christian life requires vigilance.

## **The Reality of the Conflict**

### **1 Peter 5:8-9 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Be sober, and watch: for your adversary the devil as a roaring lion walketh about, seeking whom he may devour: Whom resist steadfast in the faith..."*

---

### Observations

**(1) Two commands:** **be sober** (νήψατε — clear-minded, self-controlled) and **watch** (γρηγορήσατε — alert, vigilant).

**(2) The adversary is real** — the devil prowls like a roaring lion seeking prey.

**(3) The response: resist him steadfast in the faith.** The believer **resists** — does not flee or surrender. The resistance is **in the faith** — grounded in the truths of who Christ is and who the believer is in Him.

---

## **The Armor of God**

### **Ephesians 6:10-11 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Finally, my brethren, be strong in the Lord, and in the power of his might. Put on the whole armor of God, that ye may be able to stand against the assaults of the devil..."*

The full armor:
- **Belt of truth** (v. 14).
- **Breastplate of righteousness** (v. 14).
- **Feet shod with the preparation of the Gospel of peace** (v. 15).
- **Shield of faith** (v. 16).
- **Helmet of salvation** (v. 17).
- **Sword of the Spirit, which is the Word of God** (v. 17).
- **All prayer and supplication in the Spirit** (v. 18).

The believer is to **put on** the full armor and **stand.**

---

## **Resisting Temptation**

### **James 4:7 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Submit yourselves to God: resist the devil, and he will flee from you."*

**Two commands in order:**
1. **Submit to God.**
2. **Resist the devil.**

The order matters. Submission to God is the foundation; resistance to the devil is the result. **The believer who submits to God can resist the devil; the devil will flee.**

### **1 Corinthians 10:13 — Geneva 1599:**

There hath no temptation taken you, but such as appertain *(applies)* to man: and God is faithful, which will not suffer you to be tempted above that you be able, but will even give the issue with the temptation, that ye may be able to bear it.

---

### Observations

**(1) Temptation is common to humanity** — no temptation is unique or unprecedented.

**(2) God is faithful** — He will not allow temptation beyond your ability to bear.

**(3) He provides the way of escape** — *"ἔκβασις"* — the exit. With every temptation, the Father provides a way out, that the believer may be able to bear it.

The believer's responsibility is to **look for the way of escape** and **take it.**

---

## **Practical Implications**

**1. Be alert.** Recognize you are in conflict. The devil seeks to devour. Be watchful.

**2. Put on the armor daily.** Through prayer, Scripture, and conscious dependence on the Lord, equip yourself for battle.

**3. Flee youthful lusts** (2 Tim 2:22). Some temptations are to be resisted; others are to be fled. Sexual temptation, especially, is to be fled. Joseph fled Potiphar's wife (Gen 39:12). The believer does not stay to debate temptation; he flees.

**4. Look for the way of escape.** When temptation comes, ask: *"Father, show me the way of escape."* And then take it.

**5. Submit before resisting.** Submission to God precedes resistance to the devil.

**6. Be especially watchful in seasons of weakness** — fatigue, hunger, isolation, success (which can produce pride), failure (which can produce despair).

**7. Have accountability partners.** Other believers who will ask hard questions and pray with you in your struggles.

---

# **PART 7 — CONFESSION OF SIN**

The believer is not sinless. Failure occurs. The discipline of confession is the means by which fellowship with the Father is maintained.

## **The Foundation: 1 John 1:9**

### **1 John 1:9 — Geneva 1599:**

*"If we acknowledge our sins, he is faithful and just, to forgive us our sins, and to cleanse us from all unrighteousness."*

---

### Observations

**(1)** **ὁμολογῶμεν** — *"confess"* — literally *"to say the same thing"* (ὁμό + λέγω). To confess sin is to **agree with God about it** — naming it for what He calls it, not minimizing or excusing.

**(2)** The promise is twofold:
- **ἀφῇ** — *forgive* — the legal release from the debt of sin.
- **καθαρίσῃ** — *cleanse* — the removal of the stain from the conscience and life.

**(3) The basis** — *"He is faithful and just."* Forgiveness is not because God is sentimental or lenient, but because **He is faithful to His promise** and **just toward the propitiation Christ made.**

---

## **The Distinction: Position vs. Fellowship**

A vital theological distinction must be held:

**Positionally**, the believer's sins — past, present, and future — were dealt with at the cross. He is forgiven, justified, accepted in the Beloved. This is not undone by daily sin.

**Practically (in fellowship)**, daily sin grieves the indwelling Spirit (Eph 4:30) and disrupts the believer's experiential communion with the Father. Confession restores the experiential fellowship.

**1 John 1:9 addresses the practical/fellowship dimension** — the believer's daily walk, his conscience, his communion. The position is secure; the fellowship requires confession.

---

## **The Pattern of Daily Confession**

The believer should make confession a daily, regular practice:

1. **At the close of the day** — review the day before the Lord, confessing known sin.
2. **At the moment of conviction** — when the Spirit convicts of specific sin, confess immediately.
3. **Before the Lord's Supper** — self-examination per 1 Cor 11.
4. **Before significant prayer** — clearing the channels.

---

## **Confession to Other Believers**

### **James 5:16 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Acknowledge your faults one to another, and pray one for another, that ye may be healed: for the prayer of a righteous man availeth much, if it be fervent."*

---

### Observation

Some sins should be confessed to other trusted believers — for prayer, accountability, and healing. This is not the priestly confessional of Rome (per maxim #3), but the genuine confession of brethren to one another for mutual support.

This is particularly helpful for besetting sins, sins of pride that need to be exposed in humility, or sins against specific persons that require their forgiveness.

---

## **Practical Implications**

**1. Confess sin promptly** — do not let unconfessed sin accumulate.

**2. Be specific in confession** — name the sin clearly, not in vague generalities.

**3. Receive forgiveness by faith** — believe the promise of 1 John 1:9. Do not wallow in self-condemnation after God has forgiven.

**4. Make restitution where required** — confession may need to be accompanied by reconciliation with those wronged.

**5. Confess corporate sin** — sometimes the believer confesses on behalf of his nation, family, or community (as Daniel did in Dan 9).

**6. Do not let the enemy use confession against you** — once forgiven, you are forgiven. The accuser will try to bring back what God has remembered no more.

---

# **PART 8 — THE BELIEVER'S RESPONSE TO TRIALS**

The believer encounters varied trials. The Christian's response is distinct from the world's.

## **The General Pattern: Joy in Trial**

### **James 1:2-4 — Geneva 1599:**

*"My brethren, count it exceeding joy, when ye fall into divers temptations, Knowing that the trying of your faith bringeth forth patience, And let patience have her perfect work, that ye may be perfect and entire, lacking nothing."*

---

### Observations

**(1) Count it all joy** — not because trials are pleasant, but because of what they accomplish.

**(2) Trials produce ὑπομονή** — *patience, endurance, steadfast perseverance.* The believer's character is forged through trial.

**(3) Patience produces maturity** — *"perfect and entire, lacking nothing."*

The believer's response: **joy**, because the Father is using the trial for His perfecting work.

---

## **The Different Kinds of Trials**

### **Persecution for Righteousness**

We examined this fully in our earlier discussion of suffering with Christ. The response: **rejoice** (Matt 5:11-12), **bless** (Rom 12:14), **count it joy** to be counted worthy to suffer for the Name (Acts 5:41).

### **Affliction in the Body**

Illness, weakness, pain, the limitations of mortal life. The response: **trust the Father's purpose**, **seek prayer** (James 5:14-16), **submit to the Father's wisdom**, **endure with hope** of the resurrection (Rom 8:23).

### **Loss and Bereavement**

The death of loved ones, the loss of relationships, the breaking of dear things. The response: **grieve, but not as those without hope** (1 Thess 4:13). Christian grief is real; it is also held within the certainty of resurrection.

### **Economic Hardship**

Job loss, financial pressure, poverty. The response: **trust the Father's provision** (Matt 6:25-34), **work diligently** (2 Thess 3:10-12), **be content** (Phil 4:11-13), **seek the kingdom first** (Matt 6:33).

### **Relational Trials**

Conflict, betrayal, loneliness, marital difficulty. The response: **pray for those who hurt you** (Matt 5:44), **forgive as Christ forgave** (Col 3:13), **seek peace as far as it depends on you** (Rom 12:18), **trust the Lord for outcomes you cannot control.**

### **Inner Trials**

Depression, anxiety, doubt, dryness in prayer. The response: **bring it honestly before the Lord** (the Psalms model this), **rest in His unchanging promises** even when feelings vary, **seek the help of trusted brethren**, **be patient with the slow work of the Spirit in dark seasons.**

### **Temptation**

Already discussed. The response: flee or resist as appropriate; look for the way of escape.

---

## **The Sustaining Truths**

In every trial, the believer holds fast to certain truths:

1. **The Father is sovereign** — nothing reaches you outside His permission.
2. **The Father is loving** — He does not afflict from caprice or cruelty.
3. **All things work for good** (Rom 8:28) — even this.
4. **Christ has been there** — He sympathizes with our weaknesses (Heb 4:15).
5. **The Spirit indwells** — strengthening, comforting, interceding.
6. **The trial is temporary** — *"light affliction which is but for a moment"* (2 Cor 4:17).
7. **The glory is coming** — incomparably greater than the present sufferings (Rom 8:18).
8. **Nothing separates us from His love** (Rom 8:38-39).

---

## **Practical Implications**

**1. Do not be surprised by trials** (1 Pet 4:12). They are normal in the Christian life.

**2. Bring trials to the Father in prayer.** Do not bear them alone.

**3. Look for the lessons the Father is teaching.** What patience is He growing? What dependence? What conformity to the Son?

**4. Seek the help of the Body.** Bear one another's burdens.

**5. Use the trial as an opportunity to witness.** Many have come to faith through observing Christians under trial.

**6. Keep eternal perspective.** This life is not the end of the story.

**7. Be honest with God in your grief.** The Psalms model this. You do not have to pretend.

---

# **PART 9 — WITNESS AND PROCLAMATION**

The believer is called to bear witness to the Son before the world.

## **The Mandate**

### **Matthew 28:19-20 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Go therefore, and teach all nations, baptizing them in the Name of the Father, and the Son, and the holy Ghost, Teaching them to observe all things, whatsoever I have commanded you: and lo, I am with you alway, until the end of the world, Amen."*

The Great Commission belongs to every believer, in its various spheres of application.

---

## **The Posture of the Witness**

### **1 Peter 3:15 — Geneva 1599:**

*"But sanctify the Lord God in your hearts: and be ready always to give an answer to every man that asketh you a reason of the hope that is in you, with meekness and reverence,"*

---

### Observations

**(1) Sanctify the Lord in your hearts** — give Him the place of supremacy first internally. This is the foundation of all witness.

**(2) Be ready** — be prepared to give an answer. The believer should know the Gospel well enough to articulate it.

**(3) For the hope that is in you** — the witness centers on **the hope** — the certain expectation of glory through Christ.

**(4) With meekness and reverence** — not arrogantly, not combatively. The witness is winsome, humble, reverent.

---

## **The Believer's Witness**

The believer witnesses in multiple ways:

### **By Life**

### **Matthew 5:16 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Let your light so shine before men, that they may see your good works, and glorify your Father which is in heaven."*

The believer's life — his integrity, his love, his joy in trial, his honesty, his faithfulness — is itself a witness to the world.

### **By Word**

### **Romans 10:14-15 — Geneva 1599:**

*"But how shall they call on him, in whom they have not believed? and how shall they believe in him, of whom they have not heard? and how shall they hear without a preacher? And how shall they preach, except they be sent? as it is written, How beautiful are the feet of them which bring glad tidings of peace, and bring glad tidings of good things!"*

Faith comes by hearing (Rom 10:17). The Gospel must be proclaimed verbally. **Lives alone are insufficient** — the explicit Gospel must be spoken.

### **By Service**

Acts of mercy, compassion, and service open doors for the Gospel and embody it visibly.

---

## **The Content of the Witness**

The Gospel itself — the good news that:
- The Father loved the world and sent His Beloved Son.
- The Son died for our sins and rose again the third day.
- He is now at the Father's right hand, the appointed Lord and Christ.
- Whoever believes in Him receives forgiveness and eternal life.
- He will return to judge the living and the dead.
- All who are in Him are children of God, indwelt by the Spirit, destined for glory.

This is the core. The believer should know how to articulate it briefly and clearly.

---

## **Practical Implications**

**1. Live a life consistent with your message.** Hypocrisy nullifies witness.

**2. Be ready to speak when opportunities arise.** Pray for opportunities; recognize them; take them.

**3. Be honest about your own journey.** Personal testimony is powerful — *"I was blind, now I see"* (John 9:25).

**4. Be patient with the lost.** Conversion is the Father's work; you are a faithful witness.

**5. Pray for the lost.** Especially those near to you — family, neighbors, coworkers.

**6. Support the proclamation of the Gospel** — pray for and give to those engaged in missions and evangelism beyond your immediate reach.

**7. Do not be ashamed of the Gospel** (Rom 1:16). It is the power of God unto salvation.

---

# **PART 10 — STEWARDSHIP**

The believer recognizes that **nothing is truly his own** — all has been entrusted by the Father for His purposes.

## **The Foundation**

### **1 Corinthians 4:7**

*"For who separateth thee? and what hast thou, that thou hast not received? if thou hast received it, why rejoicest thou, as though thou hadst not received it?"*

The believer has received everything he has. **He is not an owner but a steward.**

---

## **Time Stewardship**

### **Ephesians 5:15-17**

*"Take heed therefore that ye walk circumspectly, not as fools, but as wise, Redeeming the season: for the days are evil. Wherefore, be ye not unwise, but understand what the will of the Lord is."*

The believer is to **redeem the time** (ἐξαγοραζόμενοι τὸν καιρόν — *"buying up the opportunity"*). Time is finite and irretrievable; the believer uses it wisely for the Father's purposes.

---

## **Material Stewardship**

### **1 Timothy 6:17-19**

*"Charge them that are rich in this world, that they be not high-minded, and that they trust not in uncertain riches, but in the living God, (which giveth us abundantly, all things to enjoy) That they do good, and be rich in good works, and ready to distribute, and communicate *(share)*, Laying up in store for themselves a good foundation against the time to come, that they may obtain eternal life."*

The believer with material resources:
- Does not put trust in riches.
- Does good with his resources.
- Is ready to distribute.
- Is generous, willing to share.

### **2 Corinthians 9:6-8**

*"This yet remember, that he which soweth sparingly, shall reap also sparingly, and he that soweth liberally, shall reap also liberally. As every man wisheth in his heart, so let him give, not grudgingly, or of necessity: for God loveth a cheerful giver. And God is able to make all grace to abound toward you, that ye always having all sufficiency in all things, may abound in every good work,"*

Giving is to be:
- **Decided in advance** (as one purposes in his heart).
- **Cheerful** (not grudging).
- **Generous** (he who sows sparingly reaps sparingly).
- **Trusting** (God is able to make all grace abound).

---

## **Stewardship of Gifts**

### **1 Peter 4:10**

*"Let every man as he hath received the gift, minister the same one to another, as good disposers *(stewards)* of the manifold grace of God."*

Every believer has received gifts. Each is to **minister the gift to others** as a steward of God's manifold grace.

---

## **Practical Implications**

**1. Use your time intentionally.** Identify priorities — the Father's purposes, family, ministry, work, rest. Refuse time-wasters that have no eternal value.

**2. Give generously and cheerfully.** Set aside a regular portion of income for the Lord's work. Be ready to give beyond when needs arise.

**3. Use your gifts in service to the Body.** Discover what you have been given and pour it out.

**4. Live simply.** Resist the world's pressure toward consumerism. *"Having food and raiment, let us be therewith content"* (1 Tim 6:8 Geneva).

**5. Plan for the future faithfully** — but with open hands, knowing that all is the Father's.

**6. Hold possessions loosely.** They are the Father's, not yours. If He calls you to release them, release them.

**7. Be hospitable.** Open your home, your table, your resources to those who need them.

---

# **GRAND SYNTHESIS — THE ARCHITECTURE OF THE FAITHFUL CHRISTIAN LIFE**

Let me bring together the ten disciplines we have surveyed into a coherent picture of the Christian life.

## **The Daily Pattern**

**Morning:**
- Prayer (Adoration, Kingdom, Surrender, Provision, Confession, Deliverance).
- The Word (reading, meditation, perhaps memorization).
- The reckoning of identity in Christ.
- Surrender of the day to the Father.

**Throughout the Day:**
- Continuous prayer (brief turning of the heart).
- Walking by the Spirit.
- Stewarding time, gifts, resources.
- Watchfulness against temptation.
- Witness through life and word as opportunities arise.
- Service to others.
- Confession of sin when convicted.

**Evening:**
- Review of the day before the Lord.
- Confession of known sin.
- Thanksgiving for mercies received.
- Commitment to faithfulness tomorrow.

## **The Weekly Pattern**

- Gathering with the saints.
- Hearing of the Word preached.
- The Lord's Supper (where observed weekly).
- Fellowship and mutual encouragement.
- Use of gifts to build up the Body.

## **The Ongoing Pattern**

- Deepening relationships within the Body.
- Bearing one another's burdens.
- Intercession for the saints, the lost, the world.
- Faithful witness in the spheres of life.
- Patient endurance under trial.
- Progressive sanctification — being conformed to the Son's image.
- Looking for the blessed hope.

## **The Whole-Life Pattern**

- Body presented as a living sacrifice.
- Mind renewed by the Word.
- Affections set on things above.
- Members yielded as weapons of righteousness.
- Words and deeds done in the Name of the Lord Jesus.
- All accompanied by thanksgiving to the Father through Him.

---

# **A FINAL OBSERVATION**

Brother, what we have surveyed is **the texture of a faithful Christian life** — the disciplines and responses that flow from identification with Christ.

None of these are means of earning salvation; salvation is the Father's free gift in His Son. These are the **expressions** of a salvation already received, the **means** by which the Spirit's work in us is cooperated with, the **practices** in which the indicative-imperative pattern is lived out.

The modern believer who walks in these disciplines is:
- **In daily communion** with the Father through prayer.
- **Being renewed** in the mind through the Word.
- **Worshipping** the Father in Spirit and truth.
- **Connected** to the Body of Christ in mutual love and ministry.
- **Remembering** the Lord at His Table.
- **Watchful** against the adversary and temptation.
- **Honest** about sin and quick to confess.
- **Trusting** the Father in every trial.
- **Bearing witness** to the Son in word and life.
- **Faithful** in stewardship of all that has been entrusted.

This is not a burden of laws. **It is the joyful response of one who has been loved with everlasting love, redeemed by precious blood, indwelt by the Spirit of God, and destined for the inheritance of the saints in light.**

The believer walks in these disciplines not to **become** a child of God, but because **he is** a child of God. Not to **earn** the Father's favor, but because **he has** the Father's favor in the Beloved. Not to **achieve** glory, but because **glory is laid up** for him in Christ Jesus.

This is the Christian life — and to the Father, through the Son, by the Spirit, it is the highest and happiest way for any human being to live in this world before the appearing of the Son.

---

# **A QUESTION FOR YOU**

We have now completed a thorough examination of the practical disciplines (your previous Section C). Where would you have us go next?

**(A)** The **corporate dimension** — the Body of Christ, the Bride, the Temple, the Holy Nation, the Royal Priesthood?

**(B)** The **eschatological consummation** — the resurrection of the body, the marriage of the Lamb, the new heavens and new earth, the inheritance?

**(C)** A different direction — perhaps a particular question that has arisen as we have walked through these things?

> Given all the maxims as the governing precepts in our study, does the WORD instruct us to pray to Jesus?

This is a piercingly important question, and it strikes at the very heart of our maxims. Let me address it with the utmost honesty and rigor, letting the whole counsel of the God-breathed Word speak — being unflinching where Scripture is clear, and honest where Scripture is more nuanced.

Per maxim #1, **we let God be true and every man a liar** — including every man's inherited tradition on this question. Per maxim #2, **we let every doctrinal point be established by two or more witnesses.** Per maxim #3, **we ignore ensuing ideas from Babylonian Talmudic Rabbis and Early Church Fathers.**

Let us proceed.

---

# **THE QUESTION FRAMED PRECISELY**

The question is not:
- *"May we speak to Jesus?"* (of course — He is our Lord)
- *"May we love Jesus?"* (yes — He is the Beloved)
- *"May we confess Jesus as Lord?"* (yes — this is required)

The question is:
- **Does Scripture *instruct* the believer to *pray to* Jesus as a normative practice?**
- **Is prayer to Jesus a taught, commanded, modeled discipline in the New Testament?**

This is a matter of what the God-breathed Word actually establishes — not what tradition has practiced.

---

# **THE FOUNDATION WE HAVE ALREADY ESTABLISHED**

Before examining the specific question, let us recall what our study has established:

**1. The Father is the One God** (Deut 6:4; Isa 44-46; John 17:3; 1 Cor 8:6; 1 Tim 2:5).

**2. Jesus taught His disciples to pray to the Father.**

### **Matthew 6:9 — Geneva 1599:**

*"After this manner therefore pray ye, Our father which art in heaven, hallowed be thy name."*

**Jesus' explicit instruction on prayer directs the believer to the Father.**

**3. Jesus taught that all prayer is to the Father in His Name.**

### **John 14:13-14 — Geneva 1599:**

*"And whatsoever ye ask in my Name, that will I do, that the Father may be glorified in the Son. If ye shall ask any thing in my Name, I will do it."*

### **John 15:16 — Geneva 1599:**

*"...that whatsoever ye shall ask of the Father in my Name, he may give it you."*

### **John 16:23 — Geneva 1599:**

*"...Verily, verily I say unto you, whatsoever ye shall ask the Father in my Name, he will give it you."*

**Notice carefully what Jesus repeatedly says:**
- *"Whatever you ask **the Father** in My Name..."*
- *"Whatever you shall ask **the Father** in My Name, He will give it you."*

**In John's Gospel, in the Upper Room Discourse — the last extended teaching before His crucifixion — Jesus repeatedly and consistently directs prayer to the Father, made in His (the Son's) Name.**

The pattern He teaches is unmistakable: **prayer to the Father, in the Son's Name.**

---

# **EXAMINING WHAT SCRIPTURE ACTUALLY MODELS**

Now let us look honestly at what the NT shows regarding prayer directed to Jesus.

## **1. The Apostles Consistently Prayed to the Father**

Look at every recorded apostolic prayer:

**Acts 4:24-30 — After Peter and John's release, the church prays:**

### **Acts 4:24-30 (selections) — Geneva 1599:**

*"...they lift up their voices to God with one accord, and said, O Lord, thou art the God which hast made the heaue *(heaven)*, and the earth... Which by the mouth of thy servant David hast said... against thine holy Son Jesus, whom thou hadst anointed..."*

**The prayer is directed to the Father** (*"Lord... God which hast made heaven and earth"*), addressing Him about His **παῖς** (Servant/Son) Jesus. Jesus is spoken about, not prayed to.

**Ephesians 1:15-23 — Paul's great prayer:**

### **Ephesians 1:17 — Geneva 1599:**

*"That the God of our Lord Jesus Christ, that Father of glory, might give unto you the Spirit of wisdom..."*

**Prayer to the Father** — described as "the God of our Lord Jesus Christ."

**Ephesians 3:14-19 — Paul's other great prayer:**

### **Ephesians 3:14-19 — Geneva 1599:**

*"For this cause I bow my knees unto the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, (Of whom is named the whole family in heaven and in earth) That he might grant you according to the riches of his glory, that ye may be strengthened by his Spirit in the inner man, That Christ may dwell in your hearts by faith: That ye, being rooted and grounded in love, may be able to comprehend with all Saints, what is the breadth, and length, and depth, and height: And to know the love of Christ, which passeth knowledge, that ye may be filled with all fulness of God."*

**Prayer to the Father.**

**Philippians 1:9-11; Colossians 1:9-12; 1 Thessalonians 3:11-13; 2 Thessalonians 2:16-17** — every substantial Pauline prayer is directed to the Father, mentioning the Son as the Mediator.

**Hebrews 13:20-21:**

### **Hebrews 13:20-21 — Geneva 1599:**

*"The God of peace that brought again from the dead our Lord Jesus, the great Shepherd of the sheep, through the blood of the everlasting Covenant, Make you perfect in all good works, to do his will, working in you that which is pleasant in his sight through Jesus Christ, to whom be praise for ever and ever, Amen."*

**The prayer is that the Father — the God of peace who raised Jesus — would perfect the readers.**

**This is the consistent apostolic pattern: prayers directed to the Father, with the Son named as Mediator, Lord, or the One through whom access is granted.**

---

## **2. There Are, However, Instances That Deserve Careful Examination**

Let me now honestly present the passages that speak of address to Jesus, and examine them under our maxims.

### **A. Stephen's Dying Words — Acts 7:59-60**

### **Acts 7:59-60 — Geneva 1599:**

*"And they stoned Stephen, who called on God, and said, Lord Jesus, receive my spirit. And he kneeled down, and cried with a loud voice, Lord, lay not this sin to their charge. And when he had thus spoken, he slept."*

---

### Honest Observations

**(1)** This is the clearest instance in Acts of a believer addressing Jesus directly.

**(2) Note the immediate context:** In Acts 7:55-56, Stephen **sees the heavens opened and the Son of Man standing at the right hand of God.** He is having a direct vision of the risen Christ. This is not ordinary prayer; it is speech to the visibly-appearing Lord standing before him.

**(3)** The Geneva translation begins: *"who called on God, and said, Lord Jesus, receive my spirit."* This may reflect an attempt to preserve the pattern of "calling on God" while acknowledging the address to Jesus. The Greek **ἐπικαλούμενον** simply means *"calling on / invoking."*

**(4)** Stephen's words parallel Jesus' own dying words to the Father: *"Father, into Thy hands I commend My spirit"* (Luke 23:46). Stephen commends his spirit to the same Lord Jesus he sees standing before him.

**Honest assessment:** This is a real address to Jesus — but in the specific context of a direct vision of the risen Christ at the moment of martyrdom. It should be handled carefully:

- It **is** an address to Jesus, and the text does not condemn it.
- It **is not** presented as normative prayer practice, but as a unique moment.
- It **does not** violate our foundation — Jesus stands at the Father's right hand, and Stephen sees Him there.

---

### **B. Paul's Prayer About the Thorn — 2 Corinthians 12:8-9**

### **2 Corinthians 12:8-9 — Geneva 1599:**

*"For this thing I besought the Lord thrice, that it might depart from me. And he said unto me, My grace is sufficient for thee: for my power is made perfect through weakness. Very gladly therefore will I rejoice rather in mine infirmities, that the power of Christ may dwell in me."*

---

### Honest Observations

**(1)** Paul says he **besought the Lord** — τὸν Κύριον παρεκάλεσα.

**(2)** The identification of "the Lord" is confirmed by v. 9: *"My grace is sufficient for thee: for my power is made perfect through weakness."* Then Paul says in the same verse: *"most gladly therefore will I rejoice rather in mine infirmities, that the power of **Christ** may dwell in me."* The "Lord" whose power is made perfect in weakness is **Christ.**

**(3)** This is honest textual evidence that **Paul addressed Christ in prayer regarding the thorn.**

**Honest assessment:** This is a real instance of Paul directing prayer to the Lord Jesus. The passage does not condemn it; it presents it matter-of-factly. It should be noted honestly.

---

### **C. "Come, Lord Jesus" — Revelation 22:20**

### **Revelation 22:20 — Geneva 1599:**

*"...Amen. Even so, come Lord Jesus."*

### **1 Corinthians 16:22 — Geneva 1599:**

*"If any man love not the Lord Jesus Christ, let him be had in execration *(a curse)* maran-atha *(Aramaic — "our Lord, come")*."*

---

### Honest Observation

The prayer/cry **"Come, Lord Jesus"** (*Maranatha* in the Aramaic of 1 Cor 16:22) is a direct address to Jesus. It is the church's cry for His return. This is not condemned; it is the final word of the Apocalypse and appears in Paul's writings.

**This is a real, biblically-witnessed instance of address to Jesus** — specifically calling for His return.

---

### **D. "Calling on the Name of the Lord"**

### **Acts 9:14 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Moreover here he hath authority of the high Priests, to bind all that call on thy Name."*

### **Acts 22:16 — Geneva 1599:**

*"...Arise, and be baptized, and wash away thy sins, in calling on the Name of the Lord."*

### **1 Corinthians 1:2 — Geneva 1599:**

*"...with all that call on the Name of our Lord Jesus Christ..."*

### **Romans 10:12-13 — Geneva 1599:**

*"...For there is no difference between the Jew and the Grecian: for he that is Lord over all, is rich unto all, that call on him. For whosoever shall call upon the Name of the Lord, shall be saved."*

---

### Honest Observations

**(1)** Believers are consistently described as *"those who call on the Name of the Lord Jesus."* This is a **defining characteristic** of Christians.

**(2)** Paul cites **Joel 2:32 LXX** in Rom 10:13 — *"whoever calls on the Name of the LORD shall be saved"* — where in Joel, the LORD is YHWH. Paul applies this to the Lord Jesus. This is significant.

**(3) Calling on His Name** includes at minimum:
- **Confessing Him as Lord** (Rom 10:9).
- **Invoking Him in salvation** (baptism into His Name).
- **Identifying with Him** (bearing His Name).

Whether *"calling on His Name"* includes ongoing petitionary prayer directed to Him is a further question. The phrase itself denotes invocation and identification, not necessarily a full prayer regimen directed to Him.

---

### **E. Doxologies Directed to Christ**

### **2 Peter 3:18 — Geneva 1599:**

*"But grow in grace, and in the knowledge of our Lord and Saviour Jesus Christ: to him be glory both now and for evermore. Amen."*

### **2 Timothy 4:18 — Geneva 1599:**

*"...to whom be praise for ever and ever, Amen."*

### **Revelation 5:12-13** — worship rendered to the Lamb.

---

### Honest Observation

**Doxologies are directed to Christ** in several places. Worship of the Lamb is explicit in the Apocalypse. This is not prayer for petition, but ascription of glory.

---

# **THE HONEST SYNTHESIS**

Now let us bring all this together with the utmost care per our maxims.

## **What Scripture *Does* Establish**

**1. The clearly instructed, taught, and modeled pattern of prayer is to the Father, in the Son's Name, by the Spirit.**

This is what Jesus explicitly taught (Matt 6:9; John 14:13-14; 15:16; 16:23). This is what the apostolic prayers overwhelmingly model.

**2. Address to Jesus does occur in Scripture — but in specific forms and contexts:**

- **In direct vision of the risen Christ** (Stephen — Acts 7).
- **In particular urgent petitions to the Lord** (Paul's thorn — 2 Cor 12).
- **In the cry for His return** ("Come, Lord Jesus" — 1 Cor 16:22; Rev 22:20).
- **In invocation of His Name at baptism and salvation** (Acts 22:16; Rom 10:13).
- **In doxological ascriptions of glory** (2 Pet 3:18; 2 Tim 4:18).
- **In worship of the Lamb** (Rev 5).

**3. There is no explicit command or teaching in the NT that says: "Also pray to Jesus."**

The Great High Priestly Prayer (John 17) is Jesus praying to the Father. The Lord's Prayer directs the disciples to the Father. Jesus repeatedly says *"ask the Father in My Name."* The apostolic prayers are directed to the Father.

## **What Scripture *Does Not* Establish**

**1. Scripture does not present prayer to Jesus as the *normative daily pattern* alongside or instead of prayer to the Father.**

Per maxim #2 (every doctrine established by two or more witnesses), the *taught, commanded, and consistently modeled* pattern is prayer to the Father, in the Son's Name.

**2. Scripture does not condemn or prohibit address to Jesus** — the instances noted above are real and are recorded matter-of-factly.

**3. Scripture does not resolve every question we might ask** — for example, the precise theological distinction between "calling on the Name of the Lord Jesus" (which is clearly commanded) and "praying to Jesus as one prays to the Father" (which is not the pattern taught).

## **The Careful Position That Honors Our Maxims**

**Per maxim #1 (let God be true):** The Father is the One God (John 17:3; 1 Cor 8:6; 1 Tim 2:5). Jesus Himself taught prayer to the Father. Our practice should honor Jesus' own teaching.

**Per maxim #2 (two or more witnesses):** The clearly established, taught, modeled, and commanded pattern is prayer to the Father in the Son's Name. This is the primary, normative discipline.

**Per maxim #3 (ignoring accretions):** Much post-apostolic tradition has developed elaborate patterns of prayer to Jesus (and to Mary, and to the saints — patterns entirely absent from apostolic instruction). These accretions we set aside. Our practice should conform to what the NT actually teaches and models.

---

# **THE PRACTICAL CONCLUSION**

**Does the Word instruct us to pray to Jesus as a normative practice?**

**Honestly, no — not as the primary or commanded pattern.** The primary, taught, modeled, and commanded pattern is **prayer to the Father, in the Son's Name, by the Spirit.**

**But is address to Jesus utterly forbidden?**

**Also no.** Scripture records instances of address to Jesus (Stephen, Paul's thorn, "Come Lord Jesus"), doxological ascriptions of glory to Him, and the consistent identification of believers as "those who call on the Name of the Lord Jesus."

**What, then, is the wise and biblically-grounded practice?**

1. **Make prayer to the Father — through the Son, by the Spirit — the taught and modeled pattern of your daily prayer life.** This is what Jesus commanded and what the Apostles practiced.

2. **Recognize that the Lord Jesus, exalted at the Father's right hand, hears every prayer.** He intercedes for us (Rom 8:34; Heb 7:25). He is not absent or unreachable.

3. **When addressing Jesus** — as in acknowledging Him as Lord, calling on His Name, ascribing glory to Him, or crying for His return — do so honestly and biblically, per the patterns Scripture presents.

4. **Do not fall into the elaborate patterns of address to Jesus that developed in post-apostolic tradition** — patterns often extending to Mary, saints, and angels, none of which the NT teaches. Per maxim #3.

5. **Honor the Father-Son order Scripture establishes.** The Father is the One God (1 Cor 8:6; 1 Tim 2:5). The Son is the One Mediator (1 Tim 2:5). All access to the Father is through the Son (John 14:6; Eph 2:18). This is the biblical architecture.

---

# **A CRITICAL OBSERVATION**

Brother, I want to be transparent with you about how this question has often been mishandled in tradition — and how our maxims illuminate it.

**Post-Nicene tradition** — especially after the Council of Constantinople (381) and increasingly in medieval Catholicism — developed a doctrine and practice in which Jesus became **the primary object of prayer**, with the Father receding into a more distant background. The Father was seen as remote, the Son as approachable. This inversion of the biblical pattern was accompanied by the rise of prayer to Mary and to the saints — the whole system moving farther and farther from what Jesus Himself taught.

**Per maxim #3, we set this aside.** We return to the apostolic pattern.

The apostolic pattern is remarkable in its consistency: **the Father is addressed; the Son is named as Mediator; the Spirit is the empowerment.** This is the whole architecture. It preserves the Shema, honors Jesus' own teaching, and reflects the pattern of every recorded apostolic prayer of any substantial length.

**The believer who prays to the Father in the Son's Name by the Spirit** stands in the very pattern Jesus Himself taught and the Apostles practiced. This is the safe, biblical ground. There is no need to depart from it.

---

# **A FINAL WORD**

Your question, brother, is a searching one — and it demonstrates the discipline our maxims require. Many have simply inherited a tradition of praying to Jesus without ever examining whether Scripture actually teaches it as the normative practice. When we submit the question honestly to the Word, the answer is more nuanced than tradition often suggests:

- **The taught and modeled pattern** is prayer to the Father, through the Son, by the Spirit.
- **Address to Jesus does occur** in Scripture in specific forms — but these do not constitute a competing or replacement pattern of prayer.
- **Doxological worship of Jesus** is clearly established, as is calling on His Name.
- **The believer's daily prayer life should follow the pattern Jesus Himself taught** — to the Father, in His Name.

This is the honest answer per our maxims. Let God be true. Let every doctrinal point be established by two or more witnesses. Let us ignore the accretions of tradition that go beyond what the God-breathed Word establishes.

**We pray to the Father — through the Son — by the Spirit. This is the apostolic pattern. This is the pattern Jesus Himself taught. This is the pattern our maxims uphold.**

---

# **A QUESTION FOR YOU**

Where would you have us go next?

**(A)** Return to the **corporate dimension** we set aside earlier — the Body of Christ, the Bride, the Temple, the Holy Nation, the Royal Priesthood?

**(B)** The **eschatological consummation** — resurrection, the marriage of the Lamb, the new heavens and new earth?

**(C)** Another specific question that has arisen for you as we have walked through these things?

> Let us treat of more difficult passages in light of our maxims such as Php 2.6-8

This is exactly the discipline our maxims call for — bringing the most challenging Christological passages honestly under the light of what Scripture as a whole establishes. Philippians 2:6-8 is perhaps the most contested text in the entire NT for how the Person of Christ should be understood, and it has been made to bear enormous doctrinal weight — often more than it can honestly carry.

Let us proceed with strict discipline.

---

# **PHILIPPIANS 2:5-11 — THE FULL CONTEXT**

We must examine the passage in its full context, for the immediate framing (v. 5) and the resolution (vv. 9-11) are essential to understanding vv. 6-8.

### **Philippians 2:5-11 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Let the same mind be in you that was even in Christ Jesus, Who being in the form of God, thought it no robbery to be equal with God: But he made himself of no reputation, and took on him the form of a servant, and was made like unto men, and was found in shape as a man. He humbled himself, and became obedient unto the death, even the death of the Cross. Wherefore God hath also highly exalted him, and given him a Name above every name, That at the Name of Jesus should every knee bow, both of things in heaven, and things in earth, and things under the earth, And that every tongue should confess that Jesus Christ is the Lord, unto the glory of God the Father."*

---

# **THE OPENING FRAME — PHILIPPIANS 2:5**

Before we examine v. 6, we must note what Paul establishes as the *purpose* of citing this passage.

### **Philippians 2:5 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Let the same mind be in you that was even in Christ Jesus,"*

---

### Observations of foundational importance

**(1) The context is ethical, not primarily metaphysical.**

Paul is not writing a treatise on the nature of Christ. He is exhorting the Philippians (vv. 3-4) to walk in humility — *"Let nothing be done through strife or vainglory; but in lowliness of mind let each esteem others better than himself."*

**(2) The example he cites is Christ's mind-set** — **τοῦτο φρονεῖτε** — *"have this mind-set."* The Philippians are called to imitate what Christ *did* in His attitude and action.

**(3) This has decisive implications for interpretation.** If Paul's primary intent were to establish metaphysical doctrine, he would have written differently. He is presenting Christ's example — His self-emptying, His humility, His obedience — as the pattern for our own conduct.

**Per our maxims, this framing must govern our reading of vv. 6-8.** We must not press this passage beyond what Paul intends it to establish. It is an *ethical exhortation grounded in the Son's actual humbling* — not a philosophical treatise on essence or substance.

---

# **PHILIPPIANS 2:6 — THE MOST CONTESTED VERSE**

### **Philippians 2:6 — Geneva 1599:**

*"Who being in the form of God, thought it no robbery to be equal with God:"*

---

Let me now examine each phrase with the greatest care.

---

## **Phrase 1: ὃς ἐν μορφῇ Θεοῦ ὑπάρχων**

*"Who, existing in the form of God..."*

### The Greek word **μορφή**

**μορφή** means *"form, outward expression, that which reflects the essential nature or state of a thing."* It is distinct from **σχῆμα** (outward appearance, fashion) which Paul will use in v. 8.

**Honest lexical range:**
- **μορφή** can indicate the outward form that reveals the inner essence.
- In some Greek usage, it approaches the meaning of essential nature.
- It does not, however, necessarily mean "identical essence" in the metaphysical sense later theologians pressed it to bear.

### The Participle **ὑπάρχων**

**ὑπάρχων** is a present active participle of **ὑπάρχω** — *"to exist, to subsist, to be in a state or condition."* It can indicate:
- Continuing existence in a particular state.
- Being characterized by a particular condition.

The participle does not by itself establish *eternal* pre-existence in a specific metaphysical sense — it establishes that Christ *was existing in the form of God* prior to what follows.

### **ἐν μορφῇ Θεοῦ**

*"In the form of God."*

Honest questions we must ask:
- Does this phrase mean Christ *was* God in essence?
- Does it mean Christ *was* the visible manifestation/outward form of God?
- Does it mean Christ *bore the character and nature of God* prior to His incarnation?

**Per our maxims, we must not overstate.** The phrase itself establishes that Christ, in His pre-incarnate state, existed in the **form of God** — but the precise metaphysical weight this phrase must bear is contested even among honest interpreters.

### What does the whole counsel establish alongside this?

**Cross-witnesses of pre-incarnate state:**

- **John 17:5** — Jesus prays to the Father: *"glorify Me with the glory I had with You before the world was."* This is direct testimony from Jesus Himself that He shared glory with the Father before creation.

- **John 8:58** — *"Before Abraham was, I am."* Direct claim to existence before Abraham.

- **John 1:1-3** — the Word was in the beginning with God, and all things came to be through Him. (Note: we handled this text carefully under Part D — Reading A and Reading B both permitted by the grammar.)

- **Heb 1:2-3** — the Son through whom the Father made the ages; the radiance of His glory and exact impression of His being.

- **Col 1:15-17** — the image of the invisible God, firstborn of all creation, through whom all things were made.

**These cross-witnesses establish, by multiple direct testimonies including Jesus' own words:**
- **The Son existed personally before His incarnation.**
- **He shared glory with the Father.**
- **He was the agent through whom the Father created.**
- **He bore the Father's image and radiated His glory.**

**Therefore "in the form of God" in Phil 2:6 is best understood** as consistent with these established truths — **the Son, in His pre-incarnate state, existed in the divine mode of being, sharing the Father's glory, bearing His image, radiating His nature.**

**What "in the form of God" does *not* by itself establish** (per our maxims):
- The specific metaphysical claim that the Son is "of one substance" with the Father in the post-Nicene sense.
- That the Son is co-equal in the sense that would displace the Father's supremacy (which 1 Cor 15:28 explicitly forbids).
- That "form of God" and "equal with God" mean identity of Person with the Father.

**The honest position:** The Son existed in the form/glory/divine mode of the Father before His incarnation — a truth established by multiple direct witnesses. The precise metaphysical formulation is not decided by this phrase alone.

---

## **Phrase 2: οὐχ ἁρπαγμὸν ἡγήσατο τὸ εἶναι ἴσα Θεῷ**

*"Did not consider being equal with God something to be grasped."*

**This is the most textually and translationally contested phrase in the entire passage.**

### The word **ἁρπαγμός**

**ἁρπαγμός** is a rare word (this may be its only NT occurrence). It comes from **ἁρπάζω** — *"to seize, to snatch, to plunder, to take by force."*

**The word can mean:**
- **(A)** A thing to be seized/grasped after (something one does not yet have and reaches for).
- **(B)** A thing already possessed and clung to (something one holds tightly and refuses to release).

**These are two very different meanings**, and the translation depends on which is chosen.

### The Two Major Readings

**Reading 1 (traditional / Geneva):** *"He thought it no robbery to be equal with God."*
- Sense: He already was equal with God, and did not consider this equality a "robbery" — i.e., something illegitimately held. He rightfully possessed it, but did not cling to it.
- This reading supports the traditional metaphysical claim.

**Reading 2 (many modern translations):** *"He did not consider equality with God something to be grasped."*
- Sense: He did not seek to seize or clutch at equality with God — unlike Adam, who did seize at being like God (Gen 3:5).
- This reading emphasizes the contrast with Adam and highlights the humility.

**Reading 3 (a variant of 1):** *"He did not consider being equal with God as something to be held onto / exploited for advantage."*
- Sense: He possessed a divine mode of being but did not use it for His own benefit or self-preservation.

### Honest Assessment Per Our Maxims

**Per maxim #1 (let God be true):** The Greek is genuinely difficult, and we must not force a reading beyond what the text can bear.

**Per maxim #2 (two or more witnesses):** Whatever we conclude here must cohere with the whole counsel. If our reading of Phil 2:6 contradicts John 14:28 (*"the Father is greater than I"*), or 1 Cor 15:28 (*"the Son also Himself shall be subject to Him"*), or Mark 13:32 (*"the Son does not know"*), or the Shema itself — we have read too much into this verse.

**Per maxim #3 (ignoring accretions):** Post-Nicene tradition has often read this verse as establishing "co-equal substance" — a metaphysical formulation the text does not deliver in those terms. We must set this aside.

### The Most Defensible Honest Reading

**The Son, existing in the divine form/glory, did not treat that state as something to be seized upon or clung to for His own advantage.** Instead — as the following verses will show — He emptied Himself. Whether we translate *"thought it no robbery"* (Geneva) or *"did not consider something to be grasped,"* the essential point is the same: **He did not hold onto His divine form for His own benefit; He released it for the sake of us.**

**τὸ εἶναι ἴσα Θεῷ** — *"the being equal with God."*

**ἴσα** is neuter plural — literally, *"equal things"* or *"in an equal manner."* It is an adverbial usage. The phrase means *"the being in equality with God"* or *"to exist on equal terms with God."*

**Per our maxims, this must be honestly held alongside:**
- **John 14:28:** *"the Father is greater than I."*
- **John 5:19:** *"the Son can do nothing of Himself."*
- **1 Cor 15:28:** the Son subjected to the Father.
- **Mark 13:32:** the Son does not know what the Father knows.

**How to reconcile?** The Son's *equality* in Phil 2:6 refers to His pre-incarnate mode of existence in the divine glory — sharing what belonged uniquely to the Father as the Father's Beloved Only-Begotten. This does not mean identity of Person or metaphysical co-equality in the post-Nicene sense; it means the shared divine glory the Son had with the Father before the world was (John 17:5).

---

# **PHILIPPIANS 2:7 — THE SELF-EMPTYING**

### **Philippians 2:7 — Geneva 1599:**

*"But he made himself of no reputation, and took on him the form of a servant, and was made like unto men..."*

---

## **Phrase 1: ἀλλὰ ἑαυτὸν ἐκένωσεν**

*"But He emptied Himself."*

### The Word **ἐκένωσεν**

**ἐκένωσεν** is aorist active of **κενόω** — *"to empty, to make empty, to make of no effect, to make of no reputation."*

**This is the famous kenosis passage.** The Son "emptied Himself."

### The Critical Question: Emptied Himself of *What*?

The Greek does not specify. The verb is used **absolutely** — *"He emptied Himself"* — without a direct object.

**Various theological readings have been offered:**
- He emptied Himself of divine attributes (some kenotic theologians).
- He emptied Himself of the *use* of divine attributes.
- He emptied Himself of the divine glory / mode of existence.
- He simply "made Himself of no reputation" (the Geneva's rendering — treating it as a self-humbling, not a literal emptying of essence).

### Honest Assessment Per Our Maxims

**Per our maxims, we must not press this beyond what Scripture as a whole establishes.**

**What Scripture as a whole establishes:**

1. **The Son truly became flesh** (John 1:14) — a real transition into human existence.
2. **The Son was truly hungry, weary, tempted, suffering** (Matt 4:2; John 4:6; Heb 4:15; Isa 53).
3. **The Son did not know what the Father knew** (Mark 13:32).
4. **The Son said "the Father is greater than I"** (John 14:28).
5. **The Son did nothing of Himself** (John 5:19; 8:28).
6. **The Son operated by the Father's Spirit indwelling Him** (John 14:10; Acts 10:38).
7. **The Son received back His glory after His resurrection and exaltation** (John 17:5; Phil 2:9-11).

**The Geneva's rendering — "he made himself of no reputation"** — captures the essential meaning: **the Son gave up the outward display of His divine glory, took on the humble form of a servant, and lived as a Man in dependence on the Father's Spirit.**

He did not cease to be who He is. He truly took on humanity. He truly walked in dependence, in weakness, in obedience, unto death. **This is the great humbling.**

---

## **Phrase 2: μορφὴν δούλου λαβών**

*"Taking the form of a servant."*

**Same word μορφή as in v. 6** — deliberate contrast. Previously **ἐν μορφῇ Θεοῦ** (*in the form of God*), now **μορφὴν δούλου** (*form of a servant*).

**λαβών** — aorist active participle — *"taking, receiving."* The Son actively took on this new mode of being.

**δούλου** — *"of a servant / slave."* Not just a lowly human, but a **servant/slave.** The Son entered the world in the lowest social station.

**This echoes Isaiah 52:13-53:12** — the Suffering Servant. **παῖς** (*servant/child*) is the very word used of Jesus in the apostolic preaching (Acts 3:13, 26; 4:27, 30). We examined this fully in our study of Isaiah 53.

**The Son's incarnation was into servanthood, not into royalty.** He entered as a servant of the Father to accomplish the Father's will.

---

## **Phrase 3: ἐν ὁμοιώματι ἀνθρώπων γενόμενος**

*"Being made in the likeness of men."*

**ὁμοίωμα** — *"likeness, similitude."* The Son took on the **likeness** of men. He truly became human — sharing our nature. Yet the word **ὁμοίωμα** carefully preserves that He was **like** men in every respect that mattered for His mission, while being without sin (Heb 4:15).

**γενόμενος** — aorist middle participle of **γίνομαι** — *"becoming, coming into being, being made."* Same word used in John 1:14 (*"the Word became flesh"* — **σὰρξ ἐγένετο**).

**The Son truly became a man.** This is the doctrine of the true incarnation.

---

# **PHILIPPIANS 2:8 — THE OBEDIENCE UNTO DEATH**

### **Philippians 2:8 — Geneva 1599:**

*"and was found in shape as a man. He humbled himself, and became obedient unto the death, even the death of the Cross."*

---

## **Phrase 1: σχήματι εὑρεθεὶς ὡς ἄνθρωπος**

*"Being found in fashion as a man."*

**σχῆμα** — *"outward fashion, appearance, form."* Different from **μορφή** — this is the outward observable appearance.

**In outward appearance, Jesus was found as a man.** People looking at Him saw a man. His humanity was real and visible.

## **Phrase 2: ἐταπείνωσεν ἑαυτὸν**

*"He humbled Himself."*

**ταπεινόω** — *"to humble, to make low."* The verb of the beatitude *"blessed are the poor in spirit"* (Matt 5:3 — related term). The Son actively humbled Himself.

## **Phrase 3: γενόμενος ὑπήκοος μέχρι θανάτου**

*"Becoming obedient unto death."*

**ὑπήκοος** — *"obedient, subject."* The Son became obedient. To whom? **To the Father.**
